        2.  <a href="#S4.SS3.SSS2" class="ltx_ref" title="In IV-C Patch-Type Sensitivity Analysis (RQ3) ‣ IV Comparison and Evaluation ‣ Vulnerability-Affected Versions Identification: How Far Are We?"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_title"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_ref"><span class="ltx_text">IV-C</span>2 </span><span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Results</span></span></a>
    4.  <a href="#S4.SS4" class="ltx_ref" title="In IV Comparison and Evaluation ‣ Vulnerability-Affected Versions Identification: How Far Are We?"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_title"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_ref"><span class="ltx_text">IV-D</span> </span><span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Tool Combination Analysis (RQ4)</span></span></a>
        1.  <a href="#S4.SS4.SSS1" class="ltx_ref" title="In IV-D Tool Combination Analysis (RQ4) ‣ IV Comparison and Evaluation ‣ Vulnerability-Affected Versions Identification: How Far Are We?"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_title"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_ref"><span class="ltx_text">IV-D</span>1 </span><span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Setup</span></span></a>
        2.  <a href="#S4.SS4.SSS2" class="ltx_ref" title="In IV-D Tool Combination Analysis (RQ4) ‣ IV Comparison and Evaluation ‣ Vulnerability-Affected Versions Identification: How Far Are We?"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_title"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_ref"><span class="ltx_text">IV-D</span>2 </span><span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Results</span></span></a>
6.  <a href="#S5" class="ltx_ref" title="In Vulnerability-Affected Versions Identification: How Far Are We?"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_title"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_ref">V </span><span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Discussion</span></span></a>
    1.  <a href="#S5.SS1" class="ltx_ref" title="In V Discussion ‣ Vulnerability-Affected Versions Identification: How Far Are We?"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_title"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_ref"><span class="ltx_text">V-A</span> </span><span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Challenges and Implications</span></span></a>
    2.  <a href="#S5.SS2" class="ltx_ref" title="In V Discussion ‣ Vulnerability-Affected Versions Identification: How Far Are We?"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_title"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_ref"><span class="ltx_text">V-B</span> </span><span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Practical Workarounds via Tool Combination</span></span></a>
    3.  <a href="#S5.SS3" class="ltx_ref" title="In V Discussion ‣ Vulnerability-Affected Versions Identification: How Far Are We?"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_title"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_ref"><span class="ltx_text">V-C</span> </span><span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Future Directions</span></span></a>
    4.  <a href="#S5.SS4" class="ltx_ref" title="In V Discussion ‣ Vulnerability-Affected Versions Identification: How Far Are We?"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_title"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_ref"><span class="ltx_text">V-D</span> </span><span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Threats to Validity</span></span></a>
        1.  <a href="#S5.SS4.SSS1" class="ltx_ref" title="In V-D Threats to Validity ‣ V Discussion ‣ Vulnerability-Affected Versions Identification: How Far Are We?"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_title"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_ref"><span class="ltx_text">V-D</span>1 </span><span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">External Validity</span></span></a>
        2.  <a href="#S5.SS4.SSS2" class="ltx_ref" title="In V-D Threats to Validity ‣ V Discussion ‣ Vulnerability-Affected Versions Identification: How Far Are We?"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_title"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_ref"><span class="ltx_text">V-D</span>2 </span><span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Internal Validity</span></span></a>

<div class="ltx_page_content">

# Vulnerability-Affected Versions Identification: How Far Are We? <span class="ltx_pubnotes"><span class="ltx_pubnotes_content"><span class="ltx_pubnote ltx_role_thanks"><span class="ltx_note_name">Thanks: </span><sup>1</sup>Corresponding author.</span></span></span>

<div class="ltx_authors">

<span class="ltx_creator ltx_role_author"> <span class="ltx_personname">Xingchu Chen<sup>1</sup>, Chengwei Liu<sup>2,3</sup>, Jialun Cao<sup>4</sup>, Yang Xiao<sup>11</sup>,  
Xinyue Cai<sup>1</sup>, Yeting Li<sup>1</sup>, Jingyi Shi<sup>1</sup>, Tianqi Sun<sup>1</sup>, Haiming Chen<sup>5</sup>, Wei Huo<sup>1</sup> </span><span class="ltx_author_notes"><span class="ltx_author_notes_content"> <span class="ltx_contact ltx_role_affiliation"><span class="ltx_contact_name">Affiliation: </span> </span> <span class="ltx_contact ltx_role_affiliation"><span class="ltx_contact_name">Affiliation: </span><sup>1</sup>Institute of Information Engineering, CAS; School of Cyber Security, UCAS, Beijing, China  
<sup>2</sup>Nanyang Technological University, Singapore  
<sup>3</sup>China-Singapore International Joint Research Institute (CSIJRI), Guangzhou, China  
<sup>4</sup>The Hong Kong University of Science and Technology, Hong Kong, China  
<sup>5</sup>Institute of Software, UCAS, Beijing, China  
{chenxingchu, xiaoyang, caixinyue, liyeting, shijingyi, suntianqi, huowei}@iie.ac.cn  
chengwei.liu@ntu.edu.sg, jcaoap@cse.ust.hk, chm@ios.ac.cn </span> <span class="ltx_contact ltx_role_affiliation"><span class="ltx_contact_name">Affiliation: </span> </span></span></span></span>

</div>

<div id="abstract1" class="ltx_abstract">

###### Abstract

Identifying which software versions are affected by a vulnerability is critical for patching, risk mitigation. Despite a growing body of tools, their real-world effectiveness remains unclear due to narrow evaluation scopes—often limited to early SZZ variants, outdated techniques, and small or coarse-grained datasets. In this paper, we present the first comprehensive empirical study of vulnerability-affected versions identification. We curate a high-quality benchmark of 1,128 real-world C/C++ vulnerabilities and systematically evaluate 12 representative tools from both tracing and matching paradigms across four dimensions: effectiveness at both vulnerability and version levels, root causes of false positives and negatives, sensitivity to patch characteristics, and ensemble potential. Our findings reveal fundamental limitations: no tool exceeds 45.0% accuracy, with key challenges stemming from heuristic dependence, limited semantic reasoning, and rigid matching logic. Patch structures such as add-only and cross-file changes further hinder performance. Although ensemble strategies can improve results by up to 10.1%, overall accuracy remains below 60.0%, highlighting the need for fundamentally new approaches. Moreover, our study offers actionable insights to guide tool development, combination strategies, and future research in this critical area. Finally, we release the replicated code and benchmark on our website \[<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">1</a>\] to encourage future contributions.

</div>

<div class="ltx_keywords">

###### Index Terms: 

Vulnerability-affected versions identification, SZZ Algorithm, Vulnerability Detection, Combination Strategies.

</div>

<div id="S1" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">I </span><span id="S1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Introduction</span>

<div id="S1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Software vulnerabilities continue to pose serious threats to system security. For defenders and researchers, it is critical to determine <span id="S1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">which software versions are affected by a known vulnerability</span>, referred to as <span id="S1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">vulnerability-affected version identification</span>. Accurate identification underpins a wide range of downstream applications, including vulnerability detection \[<a href="#bib.bib2" class="ltx_ref">2</a>, <a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref">3</a>, <a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref">4</a>, <a href="#bib.bib5" class="ltx_ref">5</a>\], automated patching \[<a href="#bib.bib6" class="ltx_ref">6</a>, <a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref">7</a>\], and exploitability or propagation analysis \[<a href="#bib.bib8" class="ltx_ref">8</a>, <a href="#bib.bib9" class="ltx_ref">9</a>, <a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref">10</a>\].

</div>

<div id="S1.p2" class="ltx_para">

Despite its practical importance, reliably identifying affected versions remains challenging. Public vulnerability databases such as the NVD often contain incomplete or incorrect version metadata \[<a href="#bib.bib11" class="ltx_ref">11</a>, <a href="#bib.bib12" class="ltx_ref">12</a>, <a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref">13</a>\], and many real-world vulnerabilities, especially those patched silently, are usually not documented at all \[<a href="#bib.bib14" class="ltx_ref">14</a>, <a href="#bib.bib15" class="ltx_ref">15</a>, <a href="#bib.bib16" class="ltx_ref">16</a>\]. As a result, practitioners frequently turn to analysis tools to infer affected versions retrospectively.

</div>

<div id="S1.p3" class="ltx_para">

A range of such tools has emerged, falling broadly into two categories. Tracing-based methods \[<a href="#bib.bib17" class="ltx_ref">17</a>, <a href="#bib.bib18" class="ltx_ref">18</a>, <a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref">19</a>, <a href="#bib.bib20" class="ltx_ref">20</a>, <a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref">21</a>, <a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref">22</a>\] extend the classic SZZ algorithm \[<a href="#bib.bib23" class="ltx_ref">23</a>\] to backtrack vulnerability-inducing commits and map them to released versions. Matching-based methods \[<a href="#bib.bib24" class="ltx_ref">24</a>, <a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref">13</a>, <a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref">25</a>, <a href="#bib.bib26" class="ltx_ref">26</a>, <a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref">27</a>, <a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref">28</a>, <a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">29</a>\] extract vulnerability-related code signatures and scan historical versions for semantic equivalence. However, despite promising individual results, the <span id="S1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">real-world effectiveness of these tools remains unclear</span> due to the lack of standardized, large-scale evaluation. Previous studies fall short in several ways. First, existing evaluations focus narrowly on early tracing tools, assess only a few projects. \[<a href="#bib.bib30" class="ltx_ref">30</a>, <a href="#bib.bib31" class="ltx_ref">31</a>, <a href="#bib.bib32" class="ltx_ref">32</a>\] And previous studies does not provide a thorough investigation. Second, inconsistent metrics and evaluation setups make cross-tool comparisons unreliable. Third, newer and more sophisticated tools, particularly in the matching domain, remain largely unevaluated. As a result, developers lack systematic guidance on tool selection or integration, and researchers cannot build on reliable baselines. To address this gap, our study goes substantially further by conducting stage-wise root cause analysis, quantifying the severity of failures, and identifying previously unreported challenges.

</div>

<div id="S1.p4" class="ltx_para">

Conducting a comprehensive evaluation presents two key challenges. First, building a high-quality benchmark requires curating ground-truth affected versions for real-world vulnerabilities, which is non-trivial given the inconsistent documentation, complex version histories, and the need for manual validation. Second, understanding why tools fail involves disentangling internal heuristics, code change semantics, and tool-specific assumptions, which are barely unveiled by prior studies.

</div>

<figure id="S1.F1" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="2509.03876v2/Vulnerable_Version_Study_Overview.drawio_cropped.png" id="S1.F1.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" style="aspect-ratio:476/202;" width="476" height="202" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 1: </span>Overview of Our Study</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S1.p5" class="ltx_para">

To fill these gaps, we present the first systematic empirical study on vulnerability-affected versions identification, as shown in Figure <a href="#S1.F1" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 1 ‣ I Introduction ‣ Vulnerability-Affected Versions Identification: How Far Are We?"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>. First, we build a manually curated benchmark of 1,128 real-world vulnerabilities across 132 vulnerability types from diverse C/C++ projects. Created over 1.5 months with strict validation, this dataset provides fine-grained, reproducible ground truth for tool evaluation. Second, we conduct a comprehensive comparison of 12 representative tools from both tracing and matching paradigms. The evaluation spans four dimensions: (1) effectiveness at both the vulnerability and version level, (2) root cause analysis of false positives (FPs) and false negatives (FNs), (3) sensitivity to diverse patch patterns (e.g., add-only, cross-file), and (4) potential gains from hybrid or ensemble strategies. Third, to facilitate principled diagnosis, we introduce a stage-based decomposition framework that abstracts tracing and matching workflows into comparable steps. We then analyze the strategies of each tool at every stage, and conduct joint qualitative and quantitative assessments to pinpoint systematic errors.

</div>

<div id="S1.p6" class="ltx_para">

Throughout the empirical study, we have discovered the following key findings: 1) no tool achieves more than 45.0% accuracy in identifying affected versions, raising serious concerns about their suitability for security-critical tasks; 2) The primary causes of FPs and FNs include heuristic over-reliance in tracing, insufficient semantic modeling and inflexible matching; 3) Add-only patches, cross-file changes, and multi-branch development significantly degrade performance; 4) Modular or ensemble combinations can improve accuracy by up to 10.1%, but systemic design flaws cap accuracy below 60.0%, signaling the need for fundamentally new approaches.

</div>

<div id="S1.p7" class="ltx_para">

In summary, our main contributions are as follows:

- <span id="S1.I1.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S1.I1.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Novelty</span> – We constructed the first large-scale, high-quality benchmark for vulnerability-affected version identification, covering 1,128 vulnerabilities across 132 vulnerability types from diverse C/C++ projects. This manually curated dataset, built over 1.5 months, can effectively support further reproducible and reliable evaluation of identification tools.

  </div>
- <span id="S1.I1.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S1.I1.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Rigorousness</span> – We performed a comprehensive empirical evaluation of 12 representative tools from two major categories, offering an in-depth assessment of their effectiveness across multiple dimensions.

  </div>
- <span id="S1.I1.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S1.I1.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Significance</span> – We identified key technical challenges and root causes behind tool limitations, and provided practical insights for tool improvement, ensemble design, and future research directions.

  </div>
- <span id="S1.I1.i4"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i4.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S1.I1.i4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Usefulness</span> – We release the high-quality benchmark with 1,128 vulnerabilities across 132 vulnerability types from diverse C/C++ projects to ensure transparency and encourage future study. The artifact is released on the website \[<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">1</a>\], including replicated code and the benchmark.

  </div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">II </span><span id="S2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Preliminaries and Related Work</span>

<div id="S2.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection"><span id="S2.SS1.6" class="ltx_text">II-A</span> </span><span id="S2.SS1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Vulnerability-Affected Versions Identification</span>

<div id="S2.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Given a reported vulnerability $v\hspace{0pt}u\hspace{0pt}l$ (e.g., a CVE) in a project $P$, public databases like NVD often list affected versions (e.g., via CPEs), but such metadata is frequently incomplete or inaccurate \[<a href="#bib.bib11" class="ltx_ref">11</a>, <a href="#bib.bib12" class="ltx_ref">12</a>, <a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref">13</a>\], limiting its reliability for downstream tasks \[<a href="#bib.bib6" class="ltx_ref">6</a>, <a href="#bib.bib5" class="ltx_ref">5</a>\]. We define the task of <span id="S2.SS1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">vulnerability-affected versions identification</span> as: given the set of released versions $V = {\{{v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{n}}\}}$ of $P$, identify the subset $V_{aff} \subseteq V$ where each $v_{i} \in V_{aff}$ retains vulnerable logic of $v\hspace{0pt}u\hspace{0pt}l$. Accurate identification of $V_{aff}$ is critical for managing N-day threats and enabling a timely response.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection"><span id="S2.SS2.6" class="ltx_text">II-B</span> </span><span id="S2.SS2.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Existing Identification Approaches</span>

<div id="S2.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Generally, existing approaches for identifying vulnerability-affected versions fall into two main types. Tracing-based methods build on the SZZ algorithm \[<a href="#bib.bib23" class="ltx_ref">23</a>\] to locate vulnerability-inducing commits and infer affected versions. Matching-based methods directly identify vulnerable logic across versions through static techniques (e.g., syntactic or semantic pattern matching) or dynamic analysis (e.g., fuzzing).

</div>

<div id="S2.SS2.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection"><span id="S2.SS2.SSS1.6" class="ltx_text">II-B</span>1 </span><span id="S2.SS2.SSS1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Tracing-based Approaches</span>

<div id="S2.SS2.SSS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Tracing-based methods infer affected versions by first identifying the commits that introduced a vulnerability. A number of approaches build on the SZZ algorithm \[<a href="#bib.bib23" class="ltx_ref">23</a>\], originally designed for bug-inducing commit identification. <span id="S2.SS2.SSS1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">VCCFinder</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib17" class="ltx_ref">17</a>\] was the first to adapt SZZ to vulnerability scenarios, while <span id="S2.SS2.SSS1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Lifetime</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib18" class="ltx_ref">18</a>\] improved commit identification with fine-grained heuristics. <span id="S2.SS2.SSS1.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">V-SZZ</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref">19</a>\] introduced the idea of mapping vulnerability-inducing commits to version tags, enabling affected version inference.

</div>

<div id="S2.SS2.SSS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

Numerous SZZ variants (e.g., <span id="S2.SS2.SSS1.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">AG-SZZ</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib33" class="ltx_ref">33</a>\], <span id="S2.SS2.SSS1.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">MA-SZZ</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib34" class="ltx_ref">34</a>\], <span id="S2.SS2.SSS1.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">RA-SZZ</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib35" class="ltx_ref">35</a>\], <span id="S2.SS2.SSS1.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">PR-SZZ</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib36" class="ltx_ref">36</a>\]) have been proposed to improve the precision of bug-inducing commit identification. More recent efforts explore semantic and learning-based enhancements. <span id="S2.SS2.SSS1.p2.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Neural-SZZ</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib37" class="ltx_ref">37</a>\] identifies vulnerable code using graph neural networks. <span id="S2.SS2.SSS1.p2.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Vercation</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref">21</a>\] and <span id="S2.SS2.SSS1.p2.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Sem-SZZ</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib20" class="ltx_ref">20</a>\] apply program slicing for semantic backtracking. <span id="S2.SS2.SSS1.p2.1.8" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">LLM4SZZ</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref">22</a>\] leverages large language models (LLMs) to refine both root cause localization and commit selection.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2.SS2.SSS2" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection"><span id="S2.SS2.SSS2.6" class="ltx_text">II-B</span>2 </span><span id="S2.SS2.SSS2.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Matching-based Approaches</span>

<div id="S2.SS2.SSS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

The matching-based tools are mainly designed for recurring vulnerability detection. While tools like <span id="S2.SS2.SSS2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ReDeBug</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib38" class="ltx_ref">38</a>\] were originally developed for clone detection, others such as <span id="S2.SS2.SSS2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">VISION</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref">13</a>\] and <span id="S2.SS2.SSS2.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">VerJava</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib24" class="ltx_ref">24</a>\] extend this idea specifically to identify affected versions which aligns with our evaluated scenario. These approaches evolve along multiple axes—ranging from syntactic matching to semantic analysis and structural representations. Early techniques, such as <span id="S2.SS2.SSS2.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ReDeBug</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib38" class="ltx_ref">38</a>\], Li et al. \[<a href="#bib.bib39" class="ltx_ref">39</a>\], <span id="S2.SS2.SSS2.p1.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">PatchGen<span id="S2.SS2.SSS2.p1.1.5.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_serif">\[</span><a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref">40</a><span id="S2.SS2.SSS2.p1.1.5.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_serif">\]</span></span> and <span id="S2.SS2.SSS2.p1.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">VUDDY</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">29</a>\], relied on syntactic signatures or token-level features, offering fast yet coarse-grained matching.

</div>

<div id="S2.SS2.SSS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

To enhance accuracy and reduce false positives, later methods incorporated semantic context using program slicing, taint analysis, and version filtering. Representative tools include <span id="S2.SS2.SSS2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">MVP</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref">28</a>\], <span id="S2.SS2.SSS2.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">MOVERY</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib26" class="ltx_ref">26</a>\], <span id="S2.SS2.SSS2.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Tracer</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib41" class="ltx_ref">41</a>\], <span id="S2.SS2.SSS2.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">V1SCAN</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref">27</a>\], <span id="S2.SS2.SSS2.p2.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">FIRE</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib42" class="ltx_ref">42</a>\], and <span id="S2.SS2.SSS2.p2.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">VULTURE</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib43" class="ltx_ref">43</a>\]. To further generalize matching across codebases and abstractions, structural approaches emerged. These include AST-based models like <span id="S2.SS2.SSS2.p2.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">VMUD</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib44" class="ltx_ref">44</a>\], <span id="S2.SS2.SSS2.p2.1.8" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">PATEN</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib45" class="ltx_ref">45</a>\], and <span id="S2.SS2.SSS2.p2.1.9" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">VISION</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref">13</a>\], as well as graph-based representations such as <span id="S2.SS2.SSS2.p2.1.10" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">HiddenCPG</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib46" class="ltx_ref">46</a>\]. These techniques enable richer modeling of vulnerability patterns and greater resilience to code modifications.

</div>

<div id="S2.SS2.SSS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

Besides, dynamic methods \[<a href="#bib.bib47" class="ltx_ref">47</a>, <a href="#bib.bib48" class="ltx_ref">48</a>\] use symbolic execution or proof-of-concept (PoC) exploits to validate vulnerability presence in each version. While highly precise, such methods face practical limitations due to the unavailability of PoCs and challenges in reliably building and executing historical versions. So they remain constrained in large-scale, automated settings.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection"><span id="S2.SS3.6" class="ltx_text">II-C</span> </span><span id="S2.SS3.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Existing Evaluations</span>

<div id="S2.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Several studies have examined the effectiveness of SZZ and its variants to understand their limitations and improve their design. Wen et al. \[<a href="#bib.bib30" class="ltx_ref">30</a>\] highlighted that SZZ often fails when there is no direct overlap between the fixing and inducing commits. Rezk et al. \[<a href="#bib.bib49" class="ltx_ref">49</a>\] investigated the impact of ghost commits on the SZZ algorithm and construct the evaluation dataset with developers’ information. Rosa \[<a href="#bib.bib31" class="ltx_ref">31</a>\] introduced an NLP-based method for automatically constructing evaluation datasets, while Lyu et al. \[<a href="#bib.bib32" class="ltx_ref">32</a>\] investigated the influence of ghost commits and explored the theoretical upper bound of SZZ’s accuracy. However, these evaluations primarily focus on early SZZ variants, use limited or unverified datasets, and cover only a small number of projects. As a result, they offer only a partial view of the problem and fall short of revealing broader tool effectiveness, robustness, or real-world applicability—gaps that our comprehensive empirical study aims to fill.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2.SS4" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection"><span id="S2.SS4.6" class="ltx_text">II-D</span> </span><span id="S2.SS4.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Motivation of this paper</span>

<div id="S2.SS4.p1" class="ltx_para">

Considering the exponential growth in exploited vulnerabilities, the accurate management of vulnerability information, particularly the precise delineation of affected versions, has become more critical than ever for downstream tasks, such as patch deployment and risk evaluation. Unfortunately, current vulnerability datasets often exhibit low quality and inaccurate metadata, leaving practitioners without the detail required for effective remediation. Although existing works have been proposed in the fields of inducing commit tracing based approaches and vulnerability matching based detections, none has systematically investigated how well these two types of solutions support the automated identification of vulnerable versions in real‑world settings. To address this gap, in this paper, we conduct the first comprehensive study that evaluates their practical capabilities and clarifies how far we have progressed toward fully automated, accurate vulnerable version identification, thereby illuminating directions for future research.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3" class="section ltx_section">

</figure>

<div id="S3.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection"><span id="S3.SS1.6" class="ltx_text">III-A</span> </span><span id="S3.SS1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Tool Selection</span>

<div id="S3.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

We first conduct a systematic literature review (SLR) grounded in rigorous criteria to collect related papers on these two types of approaches. Specifically, we target at top-tier software engineering and security venues (e.g., ICSE, ASE, IEEE S&P, USENIX Security) to search related papers published between May 2020 and May 2025, using keywords such as “vulnerable version”, “version range”, “SZZ”, and “recurring vulnerability.” This yielded 22 papers. After that, we further apply backward and forward snowballing to identify other relevant papers that are missed by keyword searching, and another 19 papers are included, resulting in 41 relevant papers in total for tool selection.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

As most of these techniques target C/C++ projects, which is the most concerned ecosystem in vulnerability research, we excluded tools specific to other environments (e.g., <span id="S3.SS1.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">VerJava</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib24" class="ltx_ref">24</a>\], <span id="S3.SS1.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Neural-SZZ</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib37" class="ltx_ref">37</a>\], <span id="S3.SS1.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">AFV</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref">25</a>\]) to maximize comparison targets. After that, we further filter out papers that are (1) not proposing new tools, (2) tools not being available(i.e.,<span id="S3.SS1.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">VISION</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref">13</a>\]), and (3) requiring additional information (i.e., <span id="S3.SS1.p2.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">OpenSZZ</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib50" class="ltx_ref">50</a>\] requires additional information from Jira issues \[<a href="#bib.bib51" class="ltx_ref">51</a>\]) or compilation (i.e., <span id="S3.SS1.p2.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Tracer</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib41" class="ltx_ref">41</a>\]) that are infeasible for automated identification of vulnerable versions for general vulnerabilities. As a result, 12 tools in total, 6 matching-based tools and 6 tracing-based tools, covering diverse methodologies including heuristics, semantic reasoning, and LLM-powered analysis, are finally selected as the target tools of this study. Table <a href="#S3.T1" class="ltx_ref" title="Table I ‣ III Study Design ‣ Vulnerability-Affected Versions Identification: How Far Are We?"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">I</span></a> summarizes the selected tools, their classification, citation status, and publication venues. The detailed table of the SLR is presented on our website \[<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">1</a>\].

</div>

<figure id="S3.T2" class="ltx_table">
<div id="S3.T2.4" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_center ltx_transformed_outer" style="width:310.5pt;height:59.5pt;vertical-align:-28.6pt;">
<span class="ltx_transformed_inner" style="transform:translate(-193.4pt,37.1pt) scale(0.445270204166231,0.445270204166231) ;"> </span>
<table id="S3.T2.4.1" class="ltx_tabular ltx_align_middle">

<td id="S3.T2.4.1.10.9" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left" style="padding-left: 3.7pt; padding-right: 3.7pt"><span id="S3.T2.4.1.10.9.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#E3E3E3;">128</span></td>
<td id="S3.T2.4.1.10.10" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left" style="padding-left: 3.7pt; padding-right: 3.7pt"><span id="S3.T2.4.1.10.10.1" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#E3E3E3;">Single</span></td>
</tr>

<td id="S3.T2.4.1.11.4" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_border_bb ltx_border_t" style="padding-left: 3.7pt; padding-right: 3.7pt">1,128</td>
<td id="S3.T2.4.1.11.5" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_border_bb ltx_border_t" style="padding-left: 3.7pt; padding-right: 3.7pt">1,542</td>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table II: </span><span id="S3.T2.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Overview of the Constructed Dataset.</span> #Star: GitHub stars; #CVE: collected CVEs; #Patch: total patches; #Add-only / #Del-only / #Mixed: patch types by diff composition; #Version: total affected versions; Branch: development model.</figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection"><span id="S3.SS2.6" class="ltx_text">III-B</span> </span><span id="S3.SS2.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Dataset Construction</span>

<div id="S3.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Existing datasets used for evaluating vulnerable version identification tools suffer from several key limitations: they either (1) target a single project \[<a href="#bib.bib52" class="ltx_ref">52</a>, <a href="#bib.bib32" class="ltx_ref">32</a>\] (e.g., the Linux kernel), which include a limited number of vulnerabilities (e.g., only 100 in <span id="S3.SS2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">V-SZZ</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref">19</a>\]), or (2) are with poor accuracy in their ground truths (<span id="S3.SS2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Lifetime</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib18" class="ltx_ref">18</a>\] directly takes the labeled version in NVD, which is known as inaccurate). These issues hinder the generalizability and reliability of evaluation results. To address these limitations, we construct a new benchmark that is large-scale, diverse, and accurately annotated. The construction process consists of three main steps:

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S3.SS2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_italic">1) Project Selection.</span> We select nine representative C/C++ projects based on the following criteria: (i) <span id="S3.SS2.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">popularity</span>, measured by GitHub stars, forks, and prior usage in vulnerability research; (ii) <span id="S3.SS2.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">vulnerability density</span>, ensuring the selected projects have a substantial number of reported CVEs; and (iii) <span id="S3.SS2.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">domain diversity</span>, covering a range of application areas.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S3.SS2.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_italic">2) Patch Collection.</span> For each selected project, we manually collect fixing commit of vulnerability from Jan 2020 to Dec 2024. Patch identification is primarily based on CVE references from NVD, and is cross-referenced with official security advisories or project-maintained vulnerability logs.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.p4" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S3.SS2.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_italic">3) Ground Truth Annotation.</span> We determine affected version ranges following the annotation methodology of <span id="S3.SS2.p4.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">V-SZZ</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref">19</a>\]. Then we further validated the affected versions as follows:

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.p5" class="ltx_para">

Two annotators independently identify vulnerable statements by consulting multiple sources, including CVE descriptions, vulnerability reports, GitHub issues and fixing commit. They trace the commit history of the vulnerable statements to identify the vulnerability-inducing commit. All versions between the inducing-commit and patch-commit are labeled as vulnerable. Disagreements are resolved by a third annotator through discussion and evidence inspection. Each annotator has 8 years experience in C/C++ language programming and vulnerability analysis. This multi-stage process ensures high labeling accuracy and mitigates individual bias. Among all vulnerabilities, 134(11.9%) vulnerabilities had initial annotation inconsistencies, and the Cohen’s Kappa for the labeling is 0.83.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.p6" class="ltx_para">

On average, annotating each CVE requires approximately <span id="S3.SS2.p6.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">0.5 person-hours</span>. To reduce complexity and preserve evaluation fidelity, we excluded release-candidate (RC) builds while labeling versions. The dataset can be found in our artifact. Table <a href="#S3.T2" class="ltx_ref" title="Table II ‣ III-A Tool Selection ‣ III Study Design ‣ Vulnerability-Affected Versions Identification: How Far Are We?"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">II</span></a> summarizes key statistics of our constructed dataset, which includes affected version annotations for <span id="S3.SS2.p6.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">1,128 CVEs</span>, covering a total of <span id="S3.SS2.p6.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">59,983 vulnerable versions</span>. The dataset spans <span id="S3.SS2.p6.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">132 distinct CWE types</span>, with broad coverage across vulnerability categories. To the best of our knowledge, this is the largest publicly available benchmark for vulnerability-affected version identification.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection"><span id="S3.SS3.6" class="ltx_text">III-C</span> </span><span id="S3.SS3.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Experimental Setup</span>

<div id="S3.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

We evaluate tools under default settings. Tracing-based tools follow <span id="S3.SS3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">V-SZZ</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref">19</a>\], labeling versions between introducing and fixing commits as vulnerable. For <span id="S3.SS3.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">LLM4SZZ</span>, we use llama3.1-70b \[<a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref">22</a>\]. For matching-based tools, we provide the fixing commit as input and apply each tool to the entire codebase of all historical versions. These tools extract features based on the patch and search for similar features to determine whether the version is vulnerable. We strictly follow each tool’s original setup to ensure correctness.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">IV </span><span id="S4.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Comparison and Evaluation</span>

<div id="S4.p1" class="ltx_para">

The evaluation answers four research questions (RQs):

</div>

<div id="S4.p2" class="ltx_para">

- <span id="S4.I1.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S4.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S4.I1.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">RQ1: Effectiveness Analysis.</span> <span id="S4.I1.i1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">How effective are current tools in identifying affected versions of vulnerabilities?</span>

  </div>
- <span id="S4.I1.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S4.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S4.I1.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">RQ2: Root Cause Analysis.</span> <span id="S4.I1.i2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">What are the primary causes of FPs and FNs produced by existing tools?</span>

  </div>
- <span id="S4.I1.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S4.I1.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S4.I1.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">RQ3: Patch-Type Sensitivity Analysis.</span> <span id="S4.I1.i3.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">How does identification performance vary across different patch types?</span>

  </div>
- <span id="S4.I1.i4"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S4.I1.i4.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S4.I1.i4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">RQ4: Tool Combination Analysis.</span> <span id="S4.I1.i4.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Can combining existing tools improve the overall effectiveness?</span>

  </div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table III: </span>Effectiveness of tools at the vulnerability and version levels. NM: No-Miss; NMR: No-Miss Ratio.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection"><span id="S4.SS1.SSS1.6" class="ltx_text">IV-A</span>1 </span><span id="S4.SS1.SSS1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Setup</span>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

The effectiveness is evaluated from two complementary perspectives:

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

- <span id="S4.I2.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S4.I2.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S4.I2.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Vulnerability-level Evaluation.</span> This evaluation assesses whether a tool accurately identifies the full set of affected versions for each vulnerability. A prediction is considered <span id="S4.I2.i1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">correct</span> (true positive) only if it exactly matches the ground truth (no missing or extra versions). We also define a <span id="S4.I2.i1.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">no-miss</span> case as one that contains all ground-truth versions, despite the extra ones. This distinction reflects practical trade-offs: missing affected versions may introduce security risks, while over-reporting primarily increases maintenance overhead. We report two metrics: <span id="S4.I2.i1.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Accuracy</span> ($\text{TP}/\text{Total}$), and <span id="S4.I2.i1.p1.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">No-Miss Ratio</span> (NMR = $\text{No-Miss}/\text{Total}$), where <span id="S4.I2.i1.p1.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Total</span> is the number of evaluated vulnerabilities.

  </div>
- <span id="S4.I2.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S4.I2.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S4.I2.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Version-level Evaluation.</span> Each version is evaluated independently to capture partial correctness and better reflect a tool’s generalization ability. Tools may yield both FNs and FNs, reflecting different error tendencies. We adopt standard metrics at this level: <span id="S4.I2.i2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">precision</span>, <span id="S4.I2.i2.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">recall</span>, and <span id="S4.I2.i2.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">F1-score</span>.

  </div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS2" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection"><span id="S4.SS1.SSS2.6" class="ltx_text">IV-A</span>2 </span><span id="S4.SS1.SSS2.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Results</span>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Table <a href="#S4.T3" class="ltx_ref" title="Table III ‣ IV-A Effectiveness Analysis (RQ1) ‣ IV Comparison and Evaluation ‣ Vulnerability-Affected Versions Identification: How Far Are We?"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">III</span></a> summarizes the effectiveness of each tool at both the vulnerability and version levels.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Overall Results.</span> Among all tools, <span id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">VCCFinder</span> achieves the highest performance, with an accurace of 44.9%, a No-Miss Ratio of 68.8%, and an F1-score of 77.8%. Only five tools achieve an accuracy of at least 40.0%, and only three tools reach a No-Miss Ratio above 60.0%. These results highlight a significant deficiency in the current methods’ ability to accurately identify the complete set of affected versions. Even the accuracy of the best tool (i.e., <span id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">VCCFinder</span>) is less than half, which is far beyond a reliable usage in the practice. Moreover, all evaluated tools miss part of the affected versions for at least 30.0% of the vulnerabilities, which posing substantial security risks to downstream software leading to potentially unpatched vulnerabilities.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span class="ltx_inline-block"><img id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p3.pic1" class="ltx_picture" /></span>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p4" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Vulnerability-Level vs. Version-Level Discrepancy.</span> We observe a consistent and significant gap between evaluation metrics at the vulnerability and version levels. Specifically, vulnerability-level accuracy is typically lower than the No-Miss Ratio, and both are often lower than version-level F1-scores. This discrepancy stems from fundamental differences in evaluation granularity and tolerance to partial correctness. Vulnerability-level accuracy demands an exact match with the full set of affected versions, making it highly sensitive to both false positives and false negatives. The No-Miss Ratio relaxes this by tolerating over-estimation but still penalizes any missed affected version. In contrast, version-level metrics evaluate correctness at the granularity of individual versions, offering a more nuanced and forgiving view that better aligns with practical usage scenarios. An exception is observed with <span id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p4.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">VULTURE</span>, where the NMR (55.1%) exceeds the F1-score (42.9%) due to its extremely high recall and low precision. This suggests that some tools prioritize broad coverage of potentially affected versions, achieving high recall and NMR at the cost of precision, thereby limiting their utility for precise vulnerability localization.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p5" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span class="ltx_inline-block"><img src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iUzQuU1MxLlNTUzIucDUucGljMSIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9waWN0dXJlIiBoZWlnaHQ9IjcxLjU4IiBvdmVyZmxvdz0idmlzaWJsZSIgdmVyc2lvbj0iMS4xIiB2aWV3Ym94PSIwIDAgNDc3LjM4IDcxLjU4IiB3aWR0aD0iNDc3LjM4Ij48ZyBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtc3Ryb2tlLWNvbG9yOiMwMDAwMDA7LS1sdHgtZmlsbC1jb2xvcjojMDAwMDAwOyIgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNHB0IiB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDcxLjU4KSBtYXRyaXgoMSAwIDAgLTEgMCAwKSI+PGcgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LWZpbGwtY29sb3I6I0U2RTZFNjsiIGZpbGw9IiNFNkU2RTYiIGZpbGwtb3BhY2l0eT0iMS4wIj48cGF0aCBzdHlsZT0ic3Ryb2tlOm5vbmUiIGQ9Ik0gMS45NyA1LjkxIEwgMS45NyA2NS42OCBDIDEuOTcgNjcuODUgMy43MyA2OS42MiA1LjkxIDY5LjYyIEwgNDcxLjQ3IDY5LjYyIEMgNDczLjY1IDY5LjYyIDQ3NS40MSA2Ny44NSA0NzUuNDEgNjUuNjggTCA0NzUuNDEgNS45MSBDIDQ3NS40MSAzLjczIDQ3My42NSAxLjk3IDQ3MS40NyAxLjk3IEwgNS45MSAxLjk3IEMgMy43MyAxLjk3IDEuOTcgMy43MyAxLjk3IDUuOTEgWiIgLz48L2c+PGcgZmlsbC1vcGFjaXR5PSIxLjAiIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ibWF0cml4KDEuMCAwLjAgMC4wIDEuMCA4Ljg5IDcuNDMpIj48Zm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdCBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtZm8td2lkdGg6MzMuMjJlbTstLWx0eC1mby1oZWlnaHQ6NC4yOWVtOy0tbHR4LWZvLWRlcHRoOjAuMTllbTtmb250LXNpemU6MTBwdDsiIGhlaWdodD0iNjIuMTEiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiB0cmFuc2Zvcm09Im1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDU5LjQyKSIgd2lkdGg9IjQ1OS42NyI+PHNwYW4gY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9mb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0X2NvbnRhaW5lciI+PHNwYW4gY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9mb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0X2NvbnRlbnQiPgo8c3BhbiBpZD0iUzQuU1MxLlNTUzIucDUucGljMS4xIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2lubGluZS1ibG9jayBsdHhfbWluaXBhZ2UgbHR4X2FsaWduX2JvdHRvbSIgc3R5bGU9IndpZHRoOjMzLjIyZW07Ij4KPHNwYW4gaWQ9IlM0LlNTMS5TU1MyLnA1LnBpYzEuMS4xIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X3AiPjxzcGFuIGlkPSJTNC5TUzEuU1NTMi5wNS5waWMxLjEuMS4xIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X3RleHQgbHR4X2ZvbnRfYm9sZCIgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LWZnLWNvbG9yOiMwMDAwMDA7Ij5GaW5kaW5nIDI6PHNwYW4gaWQ9IlM0LlNTMS5TU1MyLnA1LnBpYzEuMS4xLjEuMSIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF90ZXh0IGx0eF9mb250X21lZGl1bSI+CkV4aXN0aW5nIHRvb2xzIGNhbm5vdCBwcmVjaXNlbHkgaWRlbnRpZnkgdGhlIGZ1bGwgYWZmZWN0ZWQgdmVyc2lvbnMgb2YgdnVsbmVyYWJpbGl0aWVzICh3aXRoIG5vIHZlcnNpb24gb21pc3Npb25zIG9yIGZhbHNlIHBvc2l0aXZlcykuIEhvd2V2ZXIsIHRoZXkgYWNoaWV2ZSBhb id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p5.pic1" class="ltx_picture" /></span>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p6" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p6.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Methodology-Level Comparison.</span> Tracing-based tools tend to outperform matching-based ones across both evaluation levels, yielding higher summary accuracy (38.4% vs. 26.7%), No-Miss Ratio (60.9% vs. 44.6%), and F1-score (72.9% vs. 62.2%). Most tracing-based tools (except <span id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p6.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">TC-SZZ</span>) exceed 40.0% in accuracy and 55.0% in No-Miss Ratio—thresholds that are typically not reached by matching-based tools, which often prioritize precision over completeness. Version-level metrics reveal important exceptions. Matching-based tools such as <span id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p6.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">MOVERY</span> (72.8%), <span id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p6.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ReDeBug</span> (72.5%), and <span id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p6.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">FIRE</span> (72.3%) achieve F1-scores comparable to those of tracing-based tools like <span id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p6.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">LLM4SZZ</span> (72.3%). In terms of precision, matching-based tools often achieve superior results—<span id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p6.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">VUDDY</span> and <span id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p6.1.8" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">V1SCAN</span> reach 94.2% and 92.3%, though typically at the cost of recall (e.g., <span id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p6.1.9" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">VUDDY</span>: 33.4%). By contrast, tools like <span id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p6.1.10" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">VULTURE</span> prioritize recall (52.6%) over precision (36.2%), reflecting a design bias toward completeness rather than correctness.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p7" class="ltx_para">

These disparities reflect fundamental methodological differences. Matching-based tools compare code at the block/function level using syntactic or hash-based similarity, achieving high precision but remaining fragile to refactorings and minor edits. Tracing-based tools, by contrast, exploit fine-grained historical signals (e.g., <span id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p7.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">git blame</span>) to capture broader temporal context, offering better recall and resilience to superficial changes, yet potentially missing semantic dependencies beyond modified lines. We analyze these trade-offs further in RQ3.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS2.p8" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table V: </span>Overview of the key stages and strategy variants adopted by matching-based methods.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS2.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection"><span id="S4.SS2.SSS1.6" class="ltx_text">IV-B</span>1 </span><span id="S4.SS2.SSS1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Setup</span>

<div id="S4.SS2.SSS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

To understand the root causes of FPs and FNs in vulnerability-affected version identification, we adopt a mixed-method approach combining qualitative analysis and quantitative validation. We first systematically examine the key technical strategies used in tracing-based and matching-based methods, analyzing potential limitations at each stage. To validate these observations, we randomly sample 100 vulnerabilities from our dataset and evaluate identification results under representative strategies.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.SSS2" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection"><span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.6" class="ltx_text">IV-B</span>2 </span><span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Analysis of Tracing-based Tools</span>

<div id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Table <a href="#S4.T4" class="ltx_ref" title="Table IV ‣ IV-B Root Cause Analysis (RQ2) ‣ IV Comparison and Evaluation ‣ Vulnerability-Affected Versions Identification: How Far Are We?"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">IV</span></a> summarizes the strategies adopted by representative tracing-based tools across four critical stages: <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Statement Selection</span>, <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Commit Tracing</span>, <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Vulnerability-Inducing Commit Identification</span>, and <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Affected Version Inference</span>.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_italic">a) Statement Selection:</span> Most tracing-based tools (e.g., <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">V-SZZ</span>, <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">VCCFinder</span>, <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Lifetime</span>, <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p2.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">TC-SZZ</span>) rely on simple patch-based heuristics to select tracing targets, typically focusing on deleted lines or those adjacent to additions. <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p2.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">V-SZZ</span> and <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p2.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">TC-SZZ</span> are even limited to patches with deletions. While these heuristics are straightforward and easy to implement, they often fail to capture the semantic core of the vulnerability. Our manual analysis of 100 representative vulnerabilities reveals two primary sources of error in this selection process. First, when patches span multiple functions or files, most existing tools treat all code hunks uniformly, without distinguishing between semantically relevant and irrelevant edits. The lack of granularity results in the inclusion of noisy, non-critical changes as tracing candidates, which weakens the signal used for version identification. Among the analyzed cases, 49 patches involve modifications across multiple functions or files, of which 16 contain such irrelevant hunks. Although <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p2.1.8" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">LLM4SZZ</span> try to address this issue using LLM for contextual filtering, it correctly excluded the noise in only 9 of these 16 cases—indicating limited success even with advanced semantic modeling.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

Second, even in patches that modify only a single function, heuristic-based methods frequently fail to select the correct vulnerable statements. It is mainly due to their reliance on surface-level syntactic proximity rather than any principled understanding of the vulnerability’s root cause. Consequently, statement selection remains brittle in the absence of deeper semantic analysis. Across the full set of 100 cases, these limitations led to incorrect statement selection in 49 instances. More sophisticated techniques such as semantic dependency analysis (<span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">SEM-SZZ</span>) and LLM-based inference (<span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">LLM4SZZ</span>) have been proposed to mitigate this issue. Nonetheless, these methods still incorrectly identified relevant code in 39 and 28 cases, respectively, underscoring persistent challenges in semantic reasoning and contextual disambiguation.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p4" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_italic">b) Commit Tracing:</span> Most tools (e.g., <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p4.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">VCCFinder</span>, <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p4.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Lifetime</span>, <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p4.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">SEM-SZZ</span>, <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p4.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">LLM4SZZ</span>) apply one-step backward tracing, which often misses earlier commits that introduced the vulnerability. Among the 100 cases, only 70 were traceable via a single step, whereas 30 required multi-step tracing. The strategy of <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p4.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">TC-SZZ</span> tracing back to the initial commit, however, led to more false positives, with version-level precision being only 59.3%, which significantly lower than <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p4.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">V-SZZ</span>’s 74.5%. Iterative tracing methods used in <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p4.1.8" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">V-SZZ</span> attempt to address this via similarity-based heuristics but still suffer from inaccuracies: 16 were over-traced and 12 were under-traced.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p5" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p5.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_italic">c) Vulnerability-Inducing Commit Identification:</span> Once candidate commits are obtained, tools adopt varying heuristics to identify the final vulnerability-inducing commit, such as selecting the earliest or most frequently blamed commit. These heuristics, however, do not verify whether the selected commit actually introduced the vulnerability. <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p5.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">LLM4SZZ</span> try semantic verification by prompting an LLM to check vulnerability existence prior to a given commit. Nevertheless, our evaluation on 100 cases showed that <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p5.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">LLM4SZZ</span> still produced <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p5.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">12</span> FPs and <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p5.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">29</span> FNs, suggesting the difficulty of reliable semantic reasoning at the commit level.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p6" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p6.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_italic">d) Affected Version Inference:</span> In multi-branch development environments, patches may be inconsistently propagated. Tools that only analyze the <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p6.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">main</span> branch (e.g., <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p6.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">V-SZZ</span>) risk missing relevant versions. Though cross-branch matching is supported, <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p6.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">V-SZZ</span>’s simple criteria for finding duplicated patches still failed to detect relevant patches in 13 out of 100 cases.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p7" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span class="ltx_inline-block"><img src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iUzQuU1MyLlNTUzIucDcucGljMSIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9waWN0dXJlIiBoZWlnaHQ9IjEwNC43OSIgb3ZlcmZsb3c9InZpc2libGUiIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgdmlld2JveD0iMCAwIDQ3Ny4zOCAxMDQuNzkiIHdpZHRoPSI0NzcuMzgiPjxnIHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1zdHJva2UtY29sb3I6IzAwMDAwMDstLWx0eC1maWxsLWNvbG9yOiMwMDAwMDA7IiBmaWxsPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2U9IiMwMDAwMDAiIHN0cm9rZS13aWR0aD0iMC40cHQiIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMTA0Ljc5KSBtYXRyaXgoMSAwIDAgLTEgMCAwKSI+PGcgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LWZpbGwtY29sb3I6I0U2RTZFNjsiIGZpbGw9IiNFNkU2RTYiIGZpbGwtb3BhY2l0eT0iMS4wIj48cGF0aCBzdHlsZT0ic3Ryb2tlOm5vbmUiIGQ9Ik0gMS45NyA1LjkxIEwgMS45NyA5OC44OSBDIDEuOTcgMTAxLjA2IDMuNzMgMTAyLjgyIDUuOTEgMTAyLjgyIEwgNDcxLjQ3IDEwMi44MiBDIDQ3My42NSAxMDIuODIgNDc1LjQxIDEwMS4wNiA0NzUuNDEgOTguODkgTCA0NzUuNDEgNS45MSBDIDQ3NS40MSAzLjczIDQ3My42NSAxLjk3IDQ3MS40NyAxLjk3IEwgNS45MSAxLjk3IEMgMy43MyAxLjk3IDEuOTcgMy43MyAxLjk3IDUuOTEgWiIgLz48L2c+PGcgZmlsbC1vcGFjaXR5PSIxLjAiIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ibWF0cml4KDEuMCAwLjAgMC4wIDEuMCA4Ljg5IDcuNDMpIj48Zm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdCBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtZm8td2lkdGg6MzMuMjJlbTstLWx0eC1mby1oZWlnaHQ6Ni42OWVtOy0tbHR4LWZvLWRlcHRoOjAuMTllbTtmb250LXNpemU6MTBwdDsiIGhlaWdodD0iOTUuMzIiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiB0cmFuc2Zvcm09Im1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDkyLjYzKSIgd2lkdGg9IjQ1OS42NyI+PHNwYW4gY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9mb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0X2NvbnRhaW5lciI+PHNwYW4gY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9mb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0X2NvbnRlbnQiPgo8c3BhbiBpZD0iUzQuU1MyLlNTUzIucDcucGljMS4xIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2lubGluZS1ibG9jayBsdHhfbWluaXBhZ2UgbHR4X2FsaWduX2JvdHRvbSIgc3R5bGU9IndpZHRoOjMzLjIyZW07Ij4KPHNwYW4gaWQ9IlM0LlNTMi5TU1MyLnA3LnBpYzEuMS4xIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X3AiPjxzcGFuIGlkPSJTNC5TUzIuU1NTMi5wNy5waWMxLjEuMS4xIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X3RleHQgbHR4X2ZvbnRfYm9sZCIgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LWZnLWNvbG9yOiMwMDAwMDA7Ij5GaW5kaW5nIDQ6PHNwYW4gaWQ9IlM0LlNTMi5TU1MyLnA3LnBpYzEuMS4xLjEuMSIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF90ZXh0IGx0eF9mb250X21lZGl1bSI+ClRyYWNpbmctYmFzZWQgYXBwcm9hY2hlcyBhcmUgc2Vuc2l0aXZlIHRvIHRoZSBhY2N1cmFjeSBvZiBpZGVudGlmaWVkIHZ1bG5lcmFiaWxpdHkgc3RhdGVtZW50cy4gV2hpbGUgc2VtYW50aWMgYW5hbHlzaXMgYW5kIExMTXMgZW5oYW5jZSBwZXJmb3JtYW5jZSwga </div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.SSS3" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection"><span id="S4.SS2.SSS3.6" class="ltx_text">IV-B</span>3 </span><span id="S4.SS2.SSS3.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Analysis of Matching-based Tools</span>

<div id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Table <a href="#S4.T5" class="ltx_ref" title="Table V ‣ IV-B Root Cause Analysis (RQ2) ‣ IV Comparison and Evaluation ‣ Vulnerability-Affected Versions Identification: How Far Are We?"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">V</span></a> summarizes the taxonomy of matching-based tools, which typically follow two phases: <span id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">vulnerability signature construction</span> and <span id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">signature matching</span>.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_italic">a) Vulnerability Signature Construction:</span> Tools in this category extract syntactic or semantic features from known vulnerability patches to match similar instances in other versions. However, the granularity and flexibility of the extracted signatures significantly affect their effectiveness. Early tools such as <span id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ReDebug</span> and <span id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">VUDDY</span> employ coarse-grained strategies: <span id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ReDebug</span> builds sliding-window token sequences over patch hunks, while <span id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p2.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">VUDDY</span> uses the entire pre-patch function as a matching signature. These coarse-grained strategies are sensitive to unrelated code edits, resulting in frequent FNs. To improve specificity, <span id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p2.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Movery</span> and <span id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p2.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">FIRE</span> extract vulnerability-relevant statements via semantic analysis. However, both tools apply uniform extraction rules regardless of patch semantics or vulnerability type. For example, <span id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p2.1.8" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Movery</span> incorrectly extracted features in 47 cases revealing limitations in generalized semantic modeling.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p3" class="ltx_para">

Moreover, tools including <span id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Movery</span>, <span id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">V1SCAN</span>, <span id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p3.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">FIRE</span>, and <span id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p3.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">VULTURE</span> further assume that deleted lines in the patch must be present in the target code for a match. This rigid assumption fails to accommodate variants due to refactoring or non-deletion-based repairs. In our dataset, this assumption led to 55.8% of deletable-line vulnerabilities being overlooked. Notably, these tools share a structural weakness with tracing-based methods: they treat all patch modifications as equally relevant. For patches spanning multiple functions or files, this strategy often leads to semantically unrelated edits being included in the extracted signatures, thereby increasing FPs.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p4" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_italic">b) Signature Matching:</span> Most tools perform <span id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p4.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">exact matching</span> between extracted features and target code, which is brittle and contributes to high FN rates. While tools (e.g., <span id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p4.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">V1SCAN</span>, <span id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p4.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">FIRE</span>, <span id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p4.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">VULTURE</span>) relax matching using structural or semantic similarity, none verify whether the matched code actually contains the vulnerability. Consequently, these tools either fail to identify semantically equivalent vulnerabilities or misidentify safe code as vulnerable. FPs also stem from flawed signature granularity. <span id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p4.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">VUDDY</span>’s function-level signatures, for instance, produce incorrect matches in two scenarios: (1) lack of structural normalization—if the patch modifies a function’s structure (e.g., through reordering or renaming), matching may erroneously flag non-vulnerable code; (2) structural duplicates—older versions may contain functions with similar structure but semantically unrelated functions, which are wrongly matched due to lack of inter-procedural reasoning.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p5" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span class="ltx_inline-block"><img src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iUzQuU1MyLlNTUzMucDUucGljMSIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9waWN0dXJlIiBoZWlnaHQ9IjcxLjU4IiBvdmVyZmxvdz0idmlzaWJsZSIgdmVyc2lvbj0iMS4xIiB2aWV3Ym94PSIwIDAgNDc3LjM4IDcxLjU4IiB3aWR0aD0iNDc3LjM4Ij48ZyBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtc3Ryb2tlLWNvbG9yOiMwMDAwMDA7LS1sdHgtZmlsbC1jb2xvcjojMDAwMDAwOyIgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNHB0IiB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDcxLjU4KSBtYXRyaXgoMSAwIDAgLTEgMCAwKSI+PGcgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LWZpbGwtY29sb3I6I0U2RTZFNjsiIGZpbGw9IiNFNkU2RTYiIGZpbGwtb3BhY2l0eT0iMS4wIj48cGF0aCBzdHlsZT0ic3Ryb2tlOm5vbmUiIGQ9Ik0gMS45NyA1LjkxIEwgMS45NyA2NS42OCBDIDEuOTcgNjcuODUgMy43MyA2OS42MiA1LjkxIDY5LjYyIEwgNDcxLjQ3IDY5LjYyIEMgNDczLjY1IDY5LjYyIDQ3NS40MSA2Ny44NSA0NzUuNDEgNjUuNjggTCA0NzUuNDEgNS45MSBDIDQ3NS40MSAzLjczIDQ3My42NSAxLjk3IDQ3MS40NyAxLjk3IEwgNS45MSAxLjk3IEMgMy43MyAxLjk3IDEuOTcgMy43MyAxLjk3IDUuOTEgWiIgLz48L2c+PGcgZmlsbC1vcGFjaXR5PSIxLjAiIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ibWF0cml4KDEuMCAwLjAgMC4wIDEuMCA4Ljg5IDcuNDMpIj48Zm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdCBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtZm8td2lkdGg6MzMuMjJlbTstLWx0eC1mby1oZWlnaHQ6NC4yOWVtOy0tbHR4LWZvLWRlcHRoOjAuMTllbTtmb250LXNpemU6MTBwdDsiIGhlaWdodD0iNjIuMTEiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiB0cmFuc2Zvcm09Im1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDU5LjQyKSIgd2lkdGg9IjQ1OS42NyI+PHNwYW4gY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9mb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0X2NvbnRhaW5lciI+PHNwYW4gY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9mb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0X2NvbnRlbnQiPgo8c3BhbiBpZD0iUzQuU1MyLlNTUzMucDUucGljMS4xIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2lubGluZS1ibG9jayBsdHhfbWluaXBhZ2UgbHR4X2FsaWduX2JvdHRvbSIgc3R5bGU9IndpZHRoOjMzLjIyZW07Ij4KPHNwYW4gaWQ9IlM0LlNTMi5TU1MzLnA1LnBpYzEuMS4xIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X3AiPjxzcGFuIGlkPSJTNC5TUzIuU1NTMy5wNS5waWMxLjEuMS4xIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X3RleHQgbHR4X2ZvbnRfYm9sZCIgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LWZnLWNvbG9yOiMwMDAwMDA7Ij5GaW5kaW5nIDU6PHNwYW4gaWQ9IlM0LlNTMi5TU1MzLnA1LnBpYzEuMS4xLjEuMSIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF90ZXh0IGx0eF9mb250X21lZGl1bSI+ClRoZSB1c2Ugb2YgY29hcnNlLWdyYWluZWQgZmVhdHVyZXMsIGluZmxleGlibGUgbWF0Y2hpbmcgc3RyYXRlZ2llcywgYW5kIGluc3VmZmljaWVudCBzZW1hbnRpYyBtb2RlbGluZyBsaW1pdHMgdGhlIGFiaWxpdHkgb2YgbWF0Y2hpbmctYmFzZWQgbWV0aG9kcyB0byBhY2N1cmF0Z id="S4.SS2.SSS3.p5.pic1" class="ltx_picture" /></span>

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection"><span id="S4.SS3.6" class="ltx_text">IV-C</span> </span><span id="S4.SS3.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Patch-Type Sensitivity Analysis (RQ3) </span>

<figure id="S4.F2" class="ltx_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_3">
<figure id="S4.F2.sf1" class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">(a) </span>Added-only</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_3">
<figure id="S4.F2.sf2" class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">(b) </span>Del-only</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_3">
<figure id="S4.F2.sf3" class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">(c) </span>Mixed Patches</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>
</div>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 2: </span>Impact of Patch Modification Types on Tool Performance (Add-only, Del-only, Mixed).</figcaption>
</figure>

<figure id="S4.F3" class="ltx_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_3">
<figure id="S4.F3.sf1" class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">(a) </span>Single-Function</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_3">
<figure id="S4.F3.sf2" class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">(b) </span>Multi-Function Single-File</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_3">
<figure id="S4.F3.sf3" class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">(c) </span>Multi-Function Multi-File</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>
</div>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 3: </span>Tool Performance under Varying Patch Modification Scopes.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

While RQ1 and RQ2 assess tool effectiveness from overall and stage-specific perspectives, they leave open the question of how structural properties of vulnerability patches influence performance. Thus, we analyze tool robustness across three patch-level dimensions, based on their direct alignment with the internal assumptions commonly made by existing tracing- and matching-based methods. The insights also support the design and evaluation of ensemble configurations in RQ4.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.p2" class="ltx_para">

- <span id="S4.I3.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S4.I3.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S4.I3.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Type of Code Changes.</span> As observed in RQ2, most tools rely heavily on deleted lines for tracing. We therefore classify patches into *Add-only*, *Del-only*, and *Mixed* types, to evaluate sensitivity of this structural dependency.

  </div>
- <span id="S4.I3.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S4.I3.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S4.I3.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Scope of Modifications.</span> Tools typically treat all modifications uniformly, regardless of how code changes are localized. However, real-world patches may cantain a single function, span multiple functions within a file, or modify code across files. This dimension helps assess each tool’s resilience to dispersed or concentrated changes.

  </div>
- <span id="S4.I3.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S4.I3.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S4.I3.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Cross-Branch Context.</span> In multi-branch development workflows, the same vulnerability may be patched differently across branches. Although <span id="S4.I3.i3.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">V-SZZ</span> incorporates cross-branch information when identifying affected versions, the general impact of single-branch versus multi-branch patches on tool performance has not been fully explored/determined. While <span id="S4.I3.i3.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">V-SZZ</span> incorporates cross-branch information when identifying affected versions, the general impact of single-branch versus multi-branch patches on tool performance remains underexplored.

  </div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection"><span id="S4.SS3.SSS1.6" class="ltx_text">IV-C</span>1 </span><span id="S4.SS3.SSS1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Setup</span>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Based on the above design, we evaluate tools along with these three patch dimensions to understand their structural robustness and uncover potential blind spots.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS2" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection"><span id="S4.SS3.SSS2.6" class="ltx_text">IV-C</span>2 </span><span id="S4.SS3.SSS2.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Results</span>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Impact of Type of Code Changes: Add-only, Del-only, Mixed.</span> Figure <a href="#S4.F2" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 2 ‣ IV-C Patch-Type Sensitivity Analysis (RQ3) ‣ IV Comparison and Evaluation ‣ Vulnerability-Affected Versions Identification: How Far Are We?"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> shows tool performance across three patch types. Tracing-based tools perform well on *Del-only* and *Mixed* patches, but their performance drops sharply on *Add-only* patches, with version-level F1 decreasing by over 10 percentage points. This is because these tools rely on deleted lines to initiate blame tracing. Without deletions, tools like <span id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p1.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">V-SZZ</span> and <span id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p1.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">TC-SZZ</span> cannot operate. <span id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p1.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">SEM-SZZ</span> is more robust, as its dependency analysis allows itself to extract signals beyond simple line deletions. This observation reflects the semantics of patch content: deleted lines often contain the root cause, while added lines typically represent mitigation and offer limited cues for identifying the original vulnerability. Without deletions, tracing tools lack meaningful anchors to begin backward analysis.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

Matching-based tools follow a different trend. They perform best on *Mixed* patches, where both added and deleted lines provide richer context for constructing semantic signatures. Performance declines on *Add-only* and *Del-only* patches due to reduced context. In particular, *Add-only* patches are more challenging, as they contain mitigation logic rather than faulty code, making semantic matching less effective.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

Performance also varies by tool: <span id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">SEM-SZZ</span> performs best on *Add-only* patches, <span id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p3.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">LLM4SZZ</span> on *Del-only*, and <span id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p3.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">V-SZZ</span> on *Mixed* patches. This diversity highlights the complementarity of different approaches and motivates the ensemble configurations explored in RQ4.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p4" class="ltx_para">

*Del-only* patches are rare (1.3%), while *Add-only* and *Mixed* patches account for 21.3% and 77.4% of the dataset, respectively (Table <a href="#S3.T2" class="ltx_ref" title="Table II ‣ III-A Tool Selection ‣ III Study Design ‣ Vulnerability-Affected Versions Identification: How Far Are We?"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">II</span></a>). Improving robustness on these dominant types—especially *Add-only*—is therefore essential for real-world applicability.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p5" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span class="ltx_inline-block"><img src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iUzQuU1MzLlNTUzIucDUucGljMSIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9waWN0dXJlIiBoZWlnaHQ9IjcxLjU4IiBvdmVyZmxvdz0idmlzaWJsZSIgdmVyc2lvbj0iMS4xIiB2aWV3Ym94PSIwIDAgNDc3LjM4IDcxLjU4IiB3aWR0aD0iNDc3LjM4Ij48ZyBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtc3Ryb2tlLWNvbG9yOiMwMDAwMDA7LS1sdHgtZmlsbC1jb2xvcjojMDAwMDAwOyIgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNHB0IiB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDcxLjU4KSBtYXRyaXgoMSAwIDAgLTEgMCAwKSI+PGcgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LWZpbGwtY29sb3I6I0U2RTZFNjsiIGZpbGw9IiNFNkU2RTYiIGZpbGwtb3BhY2l0eT0iMS4wIj48cGF0aCBzdHlsZT0ic3Ryb2tlOm5vbmUiIGQ9Ik0gMS45NyA1LjkxIEwgMS45NyA2NS42OCBDIDEuOTcgNjcuODUgMy43MyA2OS42MiA1LjkxIDY5LjYyIEwgNDcxLjQ3IDY5LjYyIEMgNDczLjY1IDY5LjYyIDQ3NS40MSA2Ny44NSA0NzUuNDEgNjUuNjggTCA0NzUuNDEgNS45MSBDIDQ3NS40MSAzLjczIDQ3My42NSAxLjk3IDQ3MS40NyAxLjk3IEwgNS45MSAxLjk3IEMgMy43MyAxLjk3IDEuOTcgMy43MyAxLjk3IDUuOTEgWiIgLz48L2c+PGcgZmlsbC1vcGFjaXR5PSIxLjAiIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ibWF0cml4KDEuMCAwLjAgMC4wIDEuMCA4Ljg5IDcuNDMpIj48Zm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdCBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtZm8td2lkdGg6MzMuMjJlbTstLWx0eC1mby1oZWlnaHQ6NC4yOWVtOy0tbHR4LWZvLWRlcHRoOjAuMTllbTtmb250LXNpemU6MTBwdDsiIGhlaWdodD0iNjIuMTEiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiB0cmFuc2Zvcm09Im1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDU5LjQyKSIgd2lkdGg9IjQ1OS42NyI+PHNwYW4gY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9mb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0X2NvbnRhaW5lciI+PHNwYW4gY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9mb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0X2NvbnRlbnQiPgo8c3BhbiBpZD0iUzQuU1MzLlNTUzIucDUucGljMS4xIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2lubGluZS1ibG9jayBsdHhfbWluaXBhZ2UgbHR4X2FsaWduX2JvdHRvbSIgc3R5bGU9IndpZHRoOjMzLjIyZW07Ij4KPHNwYW4gaWQ9IlM0LlNTMy5TU1MyLnA1LnBpYzEuMS4xIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X3AiPjxzcGFuIGlkPSJTNC5TUzMuU1NTMi5wNS5waWMxLjEuMS4xIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X3RleHQgbHR4X2ZvbnRfYm9sZCIgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LWZnLWNvbG9yOiMwMDAwMDA7Ij5GaW5kaW5nIDY6PHNwYW4gaWQ9IlM0LlNTMy5TU1MyLnA1LnBpYzEuMS4xLjEuMSIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF90ZXh0IGx0eF9mb250X21lZGl1bSI+ClRyYWNpbmctYmFzZWQgdG9vbHMgZmFpbCBvbiA8ZW0gaWQ9IlM0LlNTMy5TU1MyLnA1LnBpYzEuMS4xLjEuMS4xIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2VtcGggbHR4X2ZvbnRfaXRhbGljIj5BZGQtb25seTwvZW0+IHBhdGNoZXMgZHVlIHRvIHRoZWlyIHJlbGlhbmNlIG9uIGRlbGV0aW9ucy4gT
